Safe Composition of Systems of Communicating Finite State Machines
Franco Barbanera, Rolf Hennicker

Abstract
The Participants-as-Interfaces (PaI) approach to system composition suggests that participants of a system may be viewed as interfaces. Given a set of systems, one participant per system is chosen to play the role of an interface. When systems are composed, the interface participants are replaced by gateways which communicate to each other by forwarding messages. The PaI-approach for systems of asynchronous communicating finite state machines (CFSMs) has been exploited in the literature for binary composition only, with a (necessarily) unique forwarding policy. In this paper we consider the case of multiple system composition when forwarding gateways are not uniquely determined and their interactions depend on specific connection policies complying with a connection model . We represent connection policies as CFSM systems and prove that a bunch of relevant communication properties (deadlock-freeness, reception-error-freeness, etc.) are preserved by PaI multicomposition , with the proviso that also the used connection policy does enjoy the communication property taken into account.



1
Introduction

Concurrent/Distributed systems are hardly – especially nowadays –
stand-alone entities. They are part of
“jigsaws” never completely finished.
Either in their design phase or after their deployment, they should be considered
as
open
and ready for interaction with their environment, and hence with other systems.
The possibility of extending and improving their functional and communication capabilities
by composing them
with other systems is also a crucial means against their obsolescence.
Compositional mechanisms and techniques are consequently an important subject for investigation.
As mentioned in
[
4
]
, system composition investigations should focus on three relevant features
of these mechanisms/techniques:

•

Conservativity:

They should alter as little as possible the single systems we compose.

•

Flexibility:

They should not be embedded into the systems we compose, i.e. they should be
“system independent”.
In particular, they should allow to consider
any
system as potentially
open
.

•

Safety:

Relevant properties of the single systems should not be “broken” by composition.

A fairly general and abstract approach to binary composition of systems
was proposed in
[
2
]
and dubbed afterwards
Participants-as-Interfaces
(PaI).
Roughly, the composition is achieved by transforming two selected participants – one per system,
say
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
, –
into coupled forwarders (gateways), provided the participants exhibit “compatible” behaviours.
The graphics in
Fig.

1
illustrates the PaI idea for the binary case.
If interface participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
of the first system
S_{1}

can receive a message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
from some participant of
S_{1}
and interface participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}

of the second system
S_{2}
can send
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
to some participant of
S_{2}
, then the gateway replacing the first interface (also called
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
) will forward the received message to the gateway for
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
.
How PaI works for multicomposition of systems will be illustrated
in
Section

2
.
It is worth remarking that the PaI approach to system composition does not expect any particular condition to be satisfied by a single participant in order to be used as an interface.

Figure 1:
The PaI idea for binary composition

Conservativity
as well as
flexibility

are definitely features of the PaI composition idea.
Conservativity holds since all participants not acting as interfaces remain untouched and flexibility holds since, in principle, any participant can play the role of an interface. This fact is independent of the concrete formalism used for protocol descriptions and system designs/implementations.

Safety
, instead, can be checked only once we take into account a
specific formalism.
Such checks were carried out in a number of papers where two relevant formalisms
for the description and verification of concurrent communicating systems were considered:
MultiParty Session Types (MPST)
[
22
,
23
]

and Communicating Finite State Machines (CFSM)
[
13
]
.
Safety of the binary PaI approach was investigated for MPST
in
[
5
]
, where a synchronous communication model was considered.
The PaI approach to multicomposition for MPST has been exploited in
[
4
,
3
]
,
again for synchronous communications.
In particular, in
[
3
]
, a restricted notion of multiple connections in a client-server setting has been considered.
For the synchronous MPST formalism used in those papers,
PaI proved to be safe.
The binary PaI approach for safety in systems of (standard) asynchronous CFSMs was taken into account in
[
2
]
, whereas
safety of PaI for a synchronous version of the CFSM formalism was
investigated in
[
7
,
8
,
9
]
, again for binary composition.

Contributions.

In the present paper we investigate safety of PaI multicomposition for the asynchronous formalism of CFSMs. For this purpose we reuse the PaI multicomposition idea of
[
4
]
but realise it – instead of the synchronous MPST framework – in the asynchronous CFSM setting which needs completely different design and proof techniques. At the same time we go beyond the binary composition of asynchronous CFSMs of
[
2
]
and study multicomposition of CFSM systems. Clearly this goes also beyond the aforementioned papers
[
7
,
8
,
9
]
dealing with binary composition of synchronous CFSMs. In particular, in the asynchronous case different communication properties, like unspecified-reception freeness, are relevant.

A crucial role in our approach to multicomposition is played by

connection policies
which can be individually chosen by the system designer on the basis of a given concrete
connection model
.
A connection model describes architectural aspects of compositions.
It specifies which forwarding links between interface roles of different systems are meaningful from a static perspective.
The concrete behavioural instantiation of such links, in terms of
which message of an interface role, say
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
, is forwarded in which state of
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
to which interface role of another system, is determined by a
connection policy which therefore also determines the construction of gateway CFSMs.
The
multicomposition
of
n
systems of CFSMs is
then simply defined by taking all CFSMs of the single systems
but replacing each CFSM of an interface participant by its gateway CFSM.
The use of connection models is methodologically important since it is more likely that a connection policy complying with a connection model will satisfy desired communication properties.
For the proofs of our safety results, however, only the specifics of the chosen connection policy is relevant.

We show that a number of relevant communication properties
(deadlock-freeness, orphan-message freeness, unspecified-reception freeness, and progress) are preserved by PaI multicomposition of CFSM systems
whenever the particular property is satisfied also by the connection policy used, which is formalised as a CFSM system itself.
Apart from orphan-message-freeness preservation we need, however, an additional assumption which requires that interface participants do not
have a state with at least one outgoing output action and one
outgoing input action,
a condition referred to in the literature as
no-mixed-state

[
16
]
.
We shall provide counterexamples illustrating the role played by the no-mixed-state condition
in guaranteeing safety of composition.
In contrast with deadlock-freeness, the stronger property of lock-freeness
will be shown (by means of a counterexample) not to be preserved in general, even in absence of of mixed-states.

Outline.

The main ideas underlying PaI multicomposition are intuitively described
in
Section

2
.
In
Section

3
we recall the definitions of communicating finite state machine, communicating system and their related notions.
There we also provide the definitions of a number of relevant communication properties.
In
Section

4
, PaI multicomposition is formally defined on the basis of the definitions
of connection policy and gateway.
Our main results are presented in
Section

5

including counterexamples spotting the role of the no-mixed-state condition and a counterexample
for lock-freeness preservation.

Section

6
concludes with a brief summary, by pointing out a few more
approaches to system composition, and with hints for future work.

2

Figure 2:

Four interface participants

In order to illustrate the idea underlying
PaI
multicomposition
1
1

1

It is of course possible to compose, two by two, several systems using binary composition,
but in that way – by looking at systems as vertices and gateway connections as undirected edges – we can get only tree-like structures of systems.
,
we consider an example of
[
4
]
with
four systems
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
,
S_{3}
and
S_{4}
.
As shown in
Fig.

2
, we have selected for each system one participant
as an interface, named
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
,
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
,
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}
.
As in
Fig.

1
, we consider here only static aspects abstracting from dynamic issues, like
the logical order of the exchanged messages,
whose representation depends on the chosen formalism.

Following the PaI approach, the composition of the four
systems above consists in replacing the participants
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
,
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
,
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}
, chosen as
interfaces, by gateways. Note that a message, like
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
in
S_{1}
sent to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
, could be forwarded (unlike the binary case) to different other gateways.
This means that a
connection policy
has to be set up
in order to appropriately define the gateways. Such a policy primarily depends on
which partner is chosen for the current message to be exchanged.

For what concerns the present example,
one could decide that message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
received by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
has to be forwarded
to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}
; the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
received by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}
to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
; the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}}
received by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}
to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}
;
the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{c}}
received by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}
to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
.
Another possible choice could be similar to the previous one but for the forwarding
of the messages
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
: the one received by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
could be forwarded now to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}

whereas the one received by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}
could be forwarded to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}
.
Such different “choices of partners”, that we formalise by introducing the notion of
connection model
, can be graphically represented, respectively, by Choice A and Choice B in

Fig.

3
.

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\[-11.38109pt]
\text{\small(Using Choice A)}&\text{\small(Using Choice B)}\end{array}

Figure 4:
Two possible PaI multicompositions via gateways

In both drawings of
Fig.

4
, the names
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
, etc. do now represent gateways.
It is important to see that even if the original CFSMs for the participants
in the single systems, like the CFSM for
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}
in
S_{3}
, are given, the connection models and the drawings in

Fig.

4

do not always determine how
a gateway CFSM, modelling the dynamic forwarding strategy, should look like.
This can be illustrated by looking at message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}}
and participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}
.
No matter whether we consider Choice A or Choice B it is
not determined when the gateway for
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}
will accept
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}}

from
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
and when from
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}
.
For instance,
a message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}}
from
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}
could be accepted by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}
only after two
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}}
’s are received from
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
.
Therefore, a given choice of partners needs, in general, to be “refined” – according to the formalism taken into account – into a specific

connection policy
taking care of the dynamic choice of partners.

This PaI approach to multicomposition has been exploited in
[
4
]

for a MPST formalism with synchronous communications.
We are now going to realise PaI multicomposition
in the context of CFSM systems with asynchronous communications.

3
Systems of Communicating Finite State Machines

Communicating Finite State Machines (CFSMs) is a widely investigated
formalism for the description and analysis of distributed systems, originally proposed in
[
13
]
.
CFSMs are a variant of finite state I/O-automata that represent processes which communicate by asynchronous exchanges of messages via FIFO channels.
We now recall (partly following
[
16
,
18
,
25
,
2
]
) the definitions of CFSM and system of CFSMs.

We assume given a countably infinite set

\mathbf{P}_{\mathfrak{U}}
of participant names (ranged over by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}},\ldots
) and a countably infinite alphabet
\mathbb{A}_{\mathfrak{U}}

of messages (ranged over by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}}
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{c}}
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{l}}
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}},\ldots
).

Definition 3.1

(CFSM)
.

Let
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbb{A}
be finite subsets of
\mathbf{P}_{\mathfrak{U}}
and
\mathbb{A}_{\mathfrak{U}}
respectively.

i)

The set
C_{\mathbf{P}}
of channels over
\mathbf{P}
is defined by
C_{\mathbf{P}}=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\mid{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\in\mathbf{P},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\neq{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\}

ii)

The set
\mathit{Act}_{\mathbf{P},\mathbb{A}}
of actions over
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbb{A}
is defined by
\textit{Act}_{\mathbf{P},\mathbb{A}}=C_{\mathbf{P}}\times\{!,?\}\times\mathbb{A}

The subject of an output action
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}
and of an input action
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}
is

{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
.

iii)

A communicating finite-state machine over
\mathbf{P}

and

\mathbb{A}

is a finite transition system given by a tuple

M=(Q,q_{0},\mathbb{A},\delta)

where
Q
is a finite set of states,
q_{0}\in Q
is the initial state, and

\delta\subseteq Q\times\textit{Act}_{\mathbf{P},\mathbb{A}}\times Q
is a set of transitions
such that all the actions have the same subject, to which we refer as the name of
M
.

We shall write
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}}
to denote a CFSM with name
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
.
Where no ambiguity arises we shall refer to a CFSM by its name.

Notice that the above definition of CFSM is generic with respect to the underlying sets

\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbb{A}
.
This is necessary, since we shall not deal with a single system of CFSMs but with an arbitrary number of systems of CFSMs that can be
composed
.
We shall write
C
and
\mathit{Act}
instead of
C_{\mathbf{P}}
and
\mathit{Act}_{\mathbf{P},\mathbb{A}}
when no ambiguity can arise.
We assume
\mathit{l},\mathit{l}^{\prime},\ldots
to range over
Act
;

\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},\ldots
to range over
\textit{Act}^{*}
(the set of finite words over
Act
), and

w,w^{\prime},\ldots
to range over
\mathbb{A}^{*}
(the set of finite words over
\mathbb{A}
).
The symbol
\varepsilon\,(\notin\mathbb{A}\cup\textit{Act})
denotes the empty word and
\mid v\mid
the lenght of a word
v\in\textit{Act}^{*}\cup\mathbb{A}^{*}
.

The transitions of a CFSM are labelled by actions; a label
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
represents
the asynchronous sending of message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
from machine
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}
to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
through channel
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
and, dually,

{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
represents the reception (consumption) of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
from channel

{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
.

Given a CFSM
M=(Q,q_{0},\mathbb{A},\delta)
,
we also define

\mathsf{in}(M)=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\mid(\_,\_\,\_?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},\_)\in\delta\}

and

\mathsf{out}(M)=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\mid(\_,\_\,\_!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},\_)\in\delta\}
.

If
M
is a CFSM with name
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
, we also write
\mathsf{in}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}})
for
\mathsf{in}(M)
and

\mathsf{out}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}})
for
\mathsf{out}(M)
.
Note that, in concrete examples, the name of a CFSM together with its input and output messages can be graphically depicted as in
Fig.

2
.

A state

q\in Q
with no outgoing transition is
final
;

q
is a
sending
(resp.
receiving
) state if it is not final and
all outgoing transitions are labelled with sending (resp. receiving) actions;

q
is a
mixed
state if there are at least two outgoing transitions such that one is labelled with a sending action and the other one is labelled with a receiving action.

A
communicating system
, called “protocol” in
[
13
]
, is a finite set of CFSMs.
In
[
16
,
18
,
25
]
the names of the CFSMs in a system are called
roles
. In the present paper we call them
participants
.

The dynamics of a system is formalised as a transition relation on configurations, where a configuration is a
pair of tuples: a tuple of states of the machines in the system and a tuple of buffers representing the content of the channels.

Definition 3.2

(Communicating system and configuration)
.

Let
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbb{A}
be as in Def.
3.1
.

i)

A communicating system (CS)
over
\mathbf{P}

and

\mathbb{A}
is a set
S=(M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}}

where

for each
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}
,

M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}=(Q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}},q_{0{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}},\mathbb{A},\delta_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}})
is a CFSM over
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbb{A}
.

ii)

A configuration of a system
S
is a pair
s=(\vec{q},\vec{w})

where

\vec{q}=(q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}}
with
q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in Q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
,
and
\vec{w}=(w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\in C}
with
w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}}\in\mathbb{A^{*}}
.

The component
\vec{q}
is the control state of the system and
q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in Q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
is the
local state of machine
M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
.
The component
\vec{w}
represents the state of the channels of the system and
w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}}\in\mathbb{A}^{*}
is the state of the channel
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}
, i.e. the messages sent from
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}
. The initial configuration of
S
is
s_{0}=(\vec{q_{0}},\vec{\varepsilon})

with
\vec{{q_{0}}}=(q_{0_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}}
.

In the following we shall often denote a communicating system
(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
by
(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}_{i}})_{i\in I}
.

Definition 3.3

(Reachable configuration)
.

Let
S
be a communicating system over
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbb{A}
, and let
s=(\vec{q},\vec{w})
and
s^{\prime}=(\vec{q^{\prime}},\vec{w^{\prime}})

be two configurations of
S
. Configuration
s^{\prime}
is reachable from
s

by firing a transition with action
\mathit{l}
, written
s\,\xrightarrow{\mathit{l}}\,s^{\prime}
, if there is
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\in\mathbb{A}

such that one of the following conditions holds:

1.

\mathit{l}={\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
and
(q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}},\mathit{l},q^{\prime}_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}})\in\delta_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}
and

a)

for all
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\neq{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}:~q^{\prime}_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}=q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
and

b)

w^{\prime}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}}=w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}}\cdot\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
and for all
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\neq{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}:~w^{\prime}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}}=w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}}
;

2.

\mathit{l}={\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
and
(q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}},\mathit{l},q^{\prime}_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}})\in\delta_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
and

a)

for all
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\neq{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}:~q^{\prime}_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}=q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
and

b)

w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\cdot w^{\prime}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}}
and for all
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\neq{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}:~w^{\prime}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}}=w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}}
.

We write
s\,\xrightarrow{}\,s^{\prime}
if there exists
\mathit{l}
such that
s\,\xrightarrow{\mathit{l}}\,s^{\prime}

and we write
s\mathrel{{\mathop{\;\;\not\!\!\longrightarrow}\limits}}\hskip 5.69054pt
if no
s^{\prime}
and no
\mathit{l}
exist with

s\,\xrightarrow{\mathit{l}}\,s^{\prime}
.
As usual, we denote the reflexive and transitive
closure of
\,\xrightarrow{}\,
by
\to^{*}
.
The set of reachable configurations of S is
\mathsf{RC}(S)=\{s\mid s_{0}\to^{*}s\}.

According to the above definition, communication happens via buffered channels following the FIFO principle.

The overall behaviour of a system can be described (at least) by the traces of configurations that are reachable from a distinguished initial one. Configurations may exhibit some pathological properties, like various forms of
deadlock
or
progress violation
, channels containing messages that will never be consumed (
orphan messages
) or just sent to a participant who is expecting another message to come (
unspecified receptions
). The goal of the analysis of
communicating systems is to check whether such kinds
of configurations are reachable or not. Although the desirable system properties are undecidable in general
[
13
]
, sufficient conditions are known that are effectively checkable
relying, for instance, on half-duplex communication
[
16
]
, on the form of network topologies
[
17
]
, or on synchronous compatibility checking
[
20
]
.

We formalise now a number of relevant communication properties for systems of CFSMs
that we shall deal with in the present paper.

Definition 3.4

(Communication properties)
.

Let
S
be a communicating system, and let
s=(\vec{q},\vec{w})
be a configuration of
S
.

i)

s
is a deadlock configuration of
S
if

\vec{w}=\vec{\varepsilon}\quad\text{and}\quad\forall{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}.~q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\text{ is a receiving state}
.

I.e. all buffers are empty, but all machines are waiting for a message.

We say that
S
is deadlock-free whenever, for any
s\in\mathsf{RC}(S)
,
s
is not a deadlock configuration.

ii)

s
is an orphan-message configuration of
S
if

\forall{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}.~q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\text{ is final}\quad\text{and}\quad\vec{w}\neq\vec{\varepsilon}
.

I.e. each machine is in a final state, but there is still at least one non-empty buffer.
We say that
S
is orphan-message free whenever, for any
s\in\mathsf{RC}(S)
,
s
is not an orphan-message configuration.

iii)

s
is an unspecified reception configuration of
S
if
\exists{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}\in\mathbf{P}
such that

a)

q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}\text{ is a receiving state}
; and

b)

\forall{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}\in\mathbf{P}.[~(q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},q^{\prime}_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}})\in\delta_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}~\Longrightarrow~(|w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}}|>0~~\wedge~~w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}}\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\cdot\mathbb{A}^{*})~]
.

I.e. there is a receiving state
q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}

which is prevented from
receiving any message from any of its buffers.
(In other words, in each channel
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
from which role
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
could consume there
is a message which cannot be received by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
in state
q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
.)
We say that
S
is reception-error free whenever, for any
s\in\mathsf{RC}(S)
,
s
is not an unspecified reception configuration.

iv)

S
satisfies the progress property if for all
s=(\vec{q},\vec{w})\in\mathsf{RC}(S)
, either there exists
s^{\prime}
such that
s\,\xrightarrow{}\,s^{\prime}

or
(\forall{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}.~q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\text{ is final})
.

v)

s
is a
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
-lock configuration of
S
if
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}
,
q_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}}
is a receiving state and

{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
does not appear as subject in any label of any transition sequence from
s

i.e.
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
remains stuck in all possible transition sequences from
s
.
We say that
S
is lock-free whenever, for each
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}
and each
s\in\mathsf{RC}(S)
,
s
is not a
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
-lock configuration.

Note that progress property (
iv
) implies deadlock-freeness.
Moreover, an unspecified reception configuration is trivially a
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
-lock for some

{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
. This immediately implies that lock-freeness implies
reception-error-freeness.
It is also straightforward to check that lock-freeness does imply both deadlock-freeness
and progress.
The other properties are mutually independent.

The above definitions of communication properties (
i
)–(
iv
) are the same as the properties considered in
[
18
]
,
though the above formulation of progress is slightly simpler but equivalent to the one in
[
18
]
.
The notions of orphan message and unspecified reception are also the same as in
[
25
]
.
The same notions of deadlock and unspecified reception are given in
[
16
]
and inspired by
[
13
]
. The deadlock notions in
[
13
]
and
[
25
]
coincide with
[
16
]
and
[
18
]
if the local CFSMs have no final states. Otherwise deadlock in
[
25
]
is weaker than deadlock above.
A still weaker notion of deadlock configuration, and hence a stronger notion of deadlock-freeness, has been suggested in
[
29
]
.
This deadlock notion has been formally related to the above
communication properties in
[
2
]
.

4
PaI Multicomposition of Communicating Systems

As described in
Section

2
, the PaI approach to multicomposition of systems
consists in replacing, in each to-be-composed system, one participant
identified as an interface by a forwarder (that we dub “gateway”).
Any participant in a system, say
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
, can be considered as an interface.
This means that we can look at the CFSM
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}

as an
abstract description of what the system expects
from a number of “outer” systems (the environment) through their respective interfaces.
Hence, any message received by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
from another participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
of the system (to which
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
belongs)
is interpreted as a message to be forwarded to some other interface
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}
among the available ones. Conversely, any message sent from
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
to another participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}

of the system (to which
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
belongs)
is interpreted as a message to be received from some other interface
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}
and to be forwarded to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
.

In order to clarify the notions introduced in this section, we present below an example from
[
4
]
, “implemented” here in the CFSM formalism.

Example 4.1

(Working example)
.

Let us consider the following four systems
2
2

2

For the sake of simplicity, the example considers only systems with two or three participants.
Our definitions and results are of course
independent of the number of participants in the single systems.
:

System-1 with participants
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
.

Participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
controls the entrance of customers in a mall (via some sensor).
As soon as a customer enters,

{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
sends a message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{start}}
to the participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
which controls a display for
advertisements. On receiving the start message,
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
displays a general advertising image.
Participant

{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}
does also control a sensor detecting emotional reactions as well as a card reader distinguishing regular from new customers. Such information, through the messages
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}}
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{rc}}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{nc}}
is sent to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
. Using that information
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
sends to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}

a customised image, depending on the kind of the customer, through message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{img}}
.

System-2 with participants
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}
.

Participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
controls an image display. Images are provided by participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}

according to some parameters sent by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
itself and depending on the reaction acquired by a sensor
driven by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}
. Images are chosen also in terms of
the kind of customers, on the basis of their cards. Participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}
is able to receive a

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{reset}}
message too, even if
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
cannot ever send it.

System-3 with participants
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3}
,
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}^{\prime}
.

Participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
controls a sensor detecting the entrance of people from a door.
Once someone enters, a message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{start}}
is sent by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
to participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3}

which turns on a light.
The reaction of who enters, detected by a sensor driven by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3}
, is sent back to

{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}
which, according to the reaction, communicates to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}^{\prime}
the
greeting to be broadcasted from the loudspeakers.

System-4 with participants
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{4}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}
.

Some sensors driven by Participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{4}
acquire the first reactions of people getting into
a hall adorned by several Christmas lights. Such reactions, sent to participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}
through a message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}}
, enable
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}
to send to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{4}
a
set of parameters
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{pars}})
allowing the latter to adjust the lights of the hall.

The behaviours of the participants of the above systems
– assuming an asynchronous model of communication – can be formalised as CFSMs. So the systems above can be formalised as the following communicating systems

S_{1}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}\in\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\}}\quad S_{2}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}\in\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\}}\quad S_{3}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}\in\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}^{\prime}\}}\quad S_{4}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}\in\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{4},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}\}}

as described, anticlockwise, in Figure
5
.

\diamond

Figure 5:
The four communicating systems formalising the systems of Example
4.1

Notation:
We use the following notation to denote the above set of communicating systems:

\{S_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,2,3,4\}}
where
S_{i}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}\in\mathbf{P}_{i}}
with

\mathbf{P}_{1}=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\}
,
\mathbf{P}_{2}=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\}
,
\mathbf{P}_{3}=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}^{\prime}\}

and
\mathbf{P}_{4}=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{4},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}\}
.

The composition of a set of systems relies on a selection of participants,
one for each system, considered as interfaces.

Definition 4.2

(Interfaces)
.

Let
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a set of communicating systems such that, for each
i\in I
,

S_{i}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}\in\mathbf{P}_{i}}
, where the
\mathbf{P}_{i}
’s are pairwise disjoint.
A set of participants
H=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathbf{P}_{i}

is a set of interfaces for
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
whenever,
for each
i\in I
,
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\in\mathbf{P}_{i}
.
An interface
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}
has
no mixed states
if the CFSM
M_{h_{i}}
in
S_{i}

has no mixed states.

Example 4.3
.

We choose
\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,2,3,4\}}
as set of interfaces for the communicating systems of Figure
5
.

\diamond

We introduce now the notion of
connection model
3
3

3

Such a notion was informally introduced in
[
4
]

in the setting of MultiParty Session Types.
, formalising what we have informally called “choice of partners” in
Section

2
.
A connection model is intended to specify the structural
(architectural) aspects of possible “reasonable” connections between interfaces of systems.
Connection models should be provided before systems are composed since they help the
system designer to avoid blatantly unreasonable compositions.
Formally, a connection model is a set of
connections
, where a connection is a triple

({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime})
in which
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}
are, respectively,
interfaces of two systems, say
S
and
S^{\prime}
, and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
is an input message
for
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
and an output message for
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}
.
Being
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
an input for
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
, this participant is supposed to receive
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
from the “inside” of
S
, i.e. from another participant of
S
.
As previously mentioned, PaI multicomposition relies on the idea
that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
can be forwarded to the interface of some other system.
The connection
({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime})
hence specifies that

{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}
is one of the possible interfaces
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
can be forwarded to.
This is sound since
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
is an output of
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}
, i.e. it is sent by
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}

to some participant of
S^{\prime}
.
The actual composition will then rely on gateways (forwarders) which comply with
the connection model taken into account.

Definition 4.4

(Connection model)
.

Let
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a set of communicating systems and let
H
be a set of interfaces for it.

i)

A connection model for
H
is a ternary relation
\text{\sc cm}\subseteq H\times\mathbb{A}_{\mathfrak{U}}\times H
such that, for each
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}\in H
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\in\mathbb{A}_{\mathfrak{U}}
,

•

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\in\mathsf{in}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}})
implies
\exists\ {\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}\in H
s.t.

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\in\mathsf{out}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime})
and
({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime})\in\text{\sc cm}

•

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\in\mathsf{out}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}})
implies
\exists\ {\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}\in H
s.t.

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\in\mathsf{in}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime})
and

({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}})\in\text{\sc cm}

where
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}\neq{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}
.

Elements of
cm
are called connections. In particular,

({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime})\in\text{\sc cm}
is called connection for
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
(from
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
to
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}
).
We also define
\mathsf{Msg}(\text{\sc cm})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\mid(\_,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},\_)\in\text{\sc cm}\}

and assume that any message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\in\mathsf{Msg}(\text{\sc cm})

occurs in one of the interfaces in
H
either as an input or as an output.

ii)

A connection model
cm
for
H
is strong if,
for each
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}\in H
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\in\mathbb{A}_{\mathfrak{U}}
,

•

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\in\mathsf{in}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}})
implies
\exists!\ {\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}\in H
s.t.
({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime})\in\text{\sc cm}

•

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\in\mathsf{out}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}})
implies
\exists!\ {\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}\in H
s.t.
({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}})\in\text{\sc cm}
.

where
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}\neq{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}
and the unique existential quantifier
‘
\exists!
’ stands for “there exists exactly one”.

Connection models can be graphically represented by diagrams,
like those used in
Fig.

3
.

Example 4.5

(Some connection models)
.

Let
H=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}\}
be the set of interfaces for the systems
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,2,3,4\}}
in
Section

2
. Fig.
3
represents the following
connection models for
H
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\text{\sc cm}_{\text{A}}&=&\{({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{c}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}})\}\\
\text{\sc cm}_{\text{B}}&=&\{({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{c}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}})\}\end{array}

Obviously, both connection models are not strong, because of the presence of the connections
({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}})
and
({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}})
.

Let us now provide a connection model for the systems in Fig.
5

with set of interfaces
H=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,2,3,4\}}
.
First we determine
\mathsf{in}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{nc}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{rc}}\}
,

\mathsf{out}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{img}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{start}}\}
,

\mathsf{in}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{img}}\}
,
\mathsf{out}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{nc}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{rc}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{pars}}\}
,

\mathsf{in}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{start}}\}
,

\mathsf{out}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}}\}
, and

\mathsf{in}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{4})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{pars}}\}
,

\mathsf{out}({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{4})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}}\}
.

A connection model for
H
is

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\text{\sc cm}&=&\{({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{4}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{start}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{img}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{nc}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}),\\
&&\hskip 5.69054pt({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{rc}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{4},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{pars}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3})\}\end{array}

The representation of
cm
is as in Fig.
6
.
Obviously, this connection model is strong.

\diamond

{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{4}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{rc}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{pars}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{start}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{nc}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{img}}

Figure 6:
A connection model for the interfaces of
Fig.

5
.

When we have more than two systems to compose, the gateways are,
in general, not uniquely determined.
In order to produce gateways out of interfaces we need to decide
which connection model we wish to take into account and how
the interfaces do actually interact “complying” with the connection model.
Once a connection model is selected, the forwarding strategy of the gateway
is still not uniquely determined if the connection model is not strong.
The reason is that in the case of at least two connectors with the same source or the same target, like
({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}})
and
({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}})
in
Example

4.5
, the gateway for
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}

has a dynamic choice when to accept message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}}
from
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}

and when from
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}
. Therefore we need further (dynamic) information
which will be provided by
connection policies
.
A connection policy is itself a communicating system which describes the dynamic choice of partners among the possible gateways
by respecting the constraints of (that is, complying with) the connection model.
Technically, we first associate a set of CFSMs (the “local connection policy set”) to each interface.
Any element of this set specifies which communications to the “outside” are allowed in which state. Technically these communications are dual to the communications of its corresponding interface.

Definition 4.6

(Local Connection Policy Set)
.

Let
cm

be a connection model for a set of interfaces
H
and let
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}\in H

with CFSM
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}=(Q,q_{0},\mathbb{A},\delta)
.

The local connection policy set of
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}
w.r.t.
cm

is the set of CFSMs
\mathsf{L}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{P}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{S}(M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\text{\sc cm})
defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{L}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{P}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{S}(M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\text{\sc cm})=\{(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{Q},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q_{0}},\mathbb{A},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\delta})\mid&\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\delta}\text{ is a minimal relation s.t. $(*)$ and $(**)$}\}\end{array}

where
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{Q}=\{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q}\mid q\in Q\}
and

(*)=q\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,q^{\prime}\in\delta\text{ implies }\exists{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in H\setminus\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}\}\,s.t.\ \accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q}\,\xrightarrow{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q^{\prime}}\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\delta}\text{ and }({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}})\in\text{\sc cm}
,

(**)=q\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,q^{\prime}\in\delta\text{ implies }\exists{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in H\setminus\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}\}\,s.t.\ \accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q}\,\xrightarrow{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q^{\prime}}\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\delta}\text{ and }({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}})\in\text{\sc cm}.

Notice that, in the above definition, each CFSM in
\mathsf{L}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{P}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{S}(M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\text{\sc cm})
has name
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}
.
Moreover,
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q}
(resp.
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}
) is to be looked at as a “decoration” of
the state
q
(resp. the name
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
).
This will enable us to immediately retrieve
q
(resp.
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
) out of
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q}
(resp.
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}
).

Notation:
In the following, for the sake of readability, we shall
write
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
(resp.
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{i}
) for
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}
(resp.
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}_{i}
).

Local connection policy sets are finite, since they contain CFSMs which only differ in the names of participants and these names belong to a finite set. Any element
(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{Q},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q}_{0},\mathbb{A},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\delta})
of
\mathsf{L}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{P}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{S}(M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\text{\sc cm})

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{0}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{1}}}}
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{1}
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{2}
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{3}
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{4}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{3}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{start}}}{}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{4}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{react}}}{}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{rc}}}{}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{2}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{img}}}{}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{nc}}}{}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{2}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{img}}}{}

does comply with the connection model
cm
, since it can only have transitions

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q}\,\xrightarrow{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q^{\prime}}\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\delta}
with
({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}})\in\text{\sc cm}

and transitions

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q}\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q^{\prime}}\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\delta}
with
({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}})\in\text{\sc cm}
,
Moreover,
\mathsf{L}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{P}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{S}(M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}},\text{\sc cm})
is a singleton if the connection model
cm
is strong.

Example 4.7

(An element of a local connection policy set)
.

Let
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}}
be the CFSM for the participant
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
of
Example

4.1

and let
cm
be the strong connection model for
H={\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,2,3,4\}}}

of Example
4.5
.
The CFSM on the right is the unique element of
\mathsf{L}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{P}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{S}(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}},\text{\sc cm})
.

\diamond

Given a connection model, a connection policy is obtained by choosing, for each interface, an element of its local connection policy set.

Definition 4.8

(Connection policy)
.

Let
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a set of communicating systems such that, for each
i\in I
,

S_{i}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}\in\mathbf{P}_{i}}
, and let

cm
be a connection model for a set of interfaces

H=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
.
A connection policy (for
H
) complying with
cm
is a communicating system
\mathbb{K}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{i}})_{i\in I}

such that, for each
i\in I
,
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{i}}\in\mathsf{L}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{P}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{S}(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}},\text{\sc cm})
.

Connection policies are made of local connection policies which, due to the conditions

(*)
and
(**)
in
Definition

4.6
, fit to the given communication model
cm
.
Consequently, in the above definition, the connection policy is said to be compliant with
cm
.
If we dropped the two requirements
(*)
and
(**)
in
Definition

4.6
we would get non-compliant connection policies.

Example 4.9

(A connection policy)
.

The following four CFSMs constitute
a connection policy for
H=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
complying with
cm
, where the
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}\!
’s
are as in Figure
5
and
cm
is the connection model of Example
4.5
.

Remark 4.10
.

A connection model can be looked at as a static and abstract description of connection policies.
In particular a connection model abstracts from the order of exchanged messages. As already pointed out above
there may be several connection policies complying with a given connection model
cm
if
cm
is not strong.
As an example assume given
three systems with the following interfaces:

0
1
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}

0
1
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h_{2}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}

0
1
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h_{3}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{w}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}

We can now consider the following (non-strong) connection model:
\text{\sc cm}=\{({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{3})\}.

It is easy to check that the connection policies
\mathbb{K}_{1}
and
\mathbb{K}_{2}
below
do both comply with
cm
.

\mathbb{K}_{1}
=
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{0}
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{1}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{1}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{3}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{0}
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{1}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{2}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{2}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{0}
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{1}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{3}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{3}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}

\mathbb{K}_{2}
=
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{0}
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{1}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{1}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{3}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{0}
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{1}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{2}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{2}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{0}
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{1}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{3}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{3}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}{}

\diamond

By now we have almost all the necessary notions to formally define the PaI multicomposition of systems of communicating systems. The only missing piece is that of
building the gateways using a connection policy.

We get a gateway essentially by transforming an interface
M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
by inserting a fresh state in between any transition.
Any input transition
q\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,q^{\prime}
(resp. output transition
q\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,q^{\prime}
) of
M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
is then transformed into two consecutive transitions

q\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,\hat{q}\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,q^{\prime}\qquad\text{(resp. $q\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}^{\prime}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,\hat{q}\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,q^{\prime}$)}

where
\hat{q}
is a fresh state and

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q}\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}^{\prime}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q^{\prime}}\,\,\ \text{(resp. $q\,\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}^{\prime}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}\,\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q^{\prime}}$)}

belonging to
the connection policy taken into account.
In the formal definition below we distinguish the fresh states by superscripting them
by the transition they are “inserted in between”.

Definition 4.11

(Gateway)
.

Assume given a connection model
cm

and two CFSMs
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}
and
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}}
such that

M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}=(Q,q_{0},\mathbb{A},\delta)
and

M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}}=(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{Q},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q_{0}},\mathbb{A},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\delta})\in\mathsf{L}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{P}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{S}(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}},\text{\sc cm})
.
The gateway
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}{{\looparrowleft}}M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}}
obtained out of
M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
and
M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}
is defined by

M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}{{\looparrowleft}}M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}}=(Q\cup\widehat{Q},q_{0},\mathbb{A},\hat{\delta})

where

-

\widehat{Q}=\bigcup_{q\in Q}\{q^{(q,l,q^{\prime})}\mid(q,l,q^{\prime})\in\delta\},

-

\widehat{\delta}=\{(q,{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},\widehat{q}),(\widehat{q},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},q^{\prime})\mid(q,{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},q^{\prime})\in\delta,(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},\ \accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q^{\prime}})\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\delta},\ \widehat{q}=q^{(q,{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},q^{\prime})}\}

{\qquad}\cup\{(q,{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},\widehat{q}),(\widehat{q},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},q^{\prime})\mid(q,{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},q^{\prime})\in\delta,\ (\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{r}}}}}!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q^{\prime}})\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\delta},\ \widehat{q}=q^{(q,{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{s}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}},q^{\prime})}\}.

We refer to
\widehat{\delta}
as
\widehat{\delta}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}
whenever
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}
is not clear from the
context; similarly for
\widehat{Q}
.

Example 4.12

(A gateway)
.

Let
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}}
be as in Example
4.1
,
and let
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{1}}
be as in the connection policy of
Example

4.9
.
The gateway
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}}{\looparrowleft}M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{1}}
is as follows.

0
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}}}}
\widehat{0}
1
\widehat{1}
2
\widehat{2}
\widehat{2}^{\prime}

4
\widehat{3}
\widehat{4}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h_{3}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{start}}}{}
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h_{1}}}}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{start}}}{}

\diamond

Definition 4.13

(Composability)
.

Let
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a set of communicating systems such that, for each
i\in I
,
S_{i}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{x}}}}\in\mathbf{P}_{i}}
.
Moreover, let
H=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a set of interfaces for it.
We say that
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is composable with respect to
H
whenever
the sets
\mathbf{P}_{i}
’s are pairwise disjoint.

Let us now describe how systems are composed on the basis of a given connection policy.

Definition 4.14

(Multicomposition of communicating systems)
.

Let
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a set of communicating systems composable with respect to
H=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}

and let
\mathbb{K}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{i}})_{i\in I}
be a connection policy
complying with a connection model
cm
for
H
.
The multicomposition of
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
with respect to
\mathbb{K}

is the communicating system

\mathcal{M}\!\mathcal{C}\!(\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I},\mathbb{K})=(M^{\prime}_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathbf{P}_{i}}

where

{\qquad\qquad}M^{\prime}_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}M_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}&\text{ if }{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\not\in\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\\[5.69054pt]
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}{{\looparrowleft}\,}M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{i}}&\text{ if }{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}={\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\text{ with $i\in I$}\end{array}\right.

Note that the CFSMs of a composition are CFSMs over
\mathbf{P}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathbf{P}_{i}

and
\mathbb{A}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathbb{A}_{i}
.
Graphically, the architectural structure of a multicomposition via gateways can be shown as in
Fig.

4
.

5
On the Preservation of Communication Properties

The main result of the present paper is the safety of PaI multicomposition of CFSM
systems for all communication properties of
Definition

3.4
but lock-freeness.
Apart from orphan-message-freeness we need the no-mixed-state assumption for interfaces to obtain the preservation results.

Theorem 5.1

(Safety of PaI multicomposition of CFSM systems)
.

Let
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a set of communicating systems composable with respect to a set

H=\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}

of interfaces with no mixed states (cf.
Definition

4.2
) and
let
\mathbb{K}
be a connection policy for
H
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be
either the property of deadlock-freeness or
reception-error-freeness or progress.
If
\mathcal{P}
holds for each
S_{i}
with
i\in I
and for
\mathbb{K}
,
then
\mathcal{P}
holds for
S=\mathcal{M}\!\mathcal{C}\!(\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I},\mathbb{K})
.
Moreover, the above holds also if the no-mixed-state condition is removed and

\mathcal{P}
is orphan-message-freeness.

Remark 5.2
.

The above result about safety of multicomposition is actually independent of a concrete connection model.
Considering connection policies which comply with a connection model is, however,
helpful at the design stage of the multicomposition and
enhances the possibility of getting connection policies which
satisfy communication properties and hence support the preservation of communication properties of the composed systems.

\diamond

Theorem

5.1
can be proved for each property
\mathcal{P}
separately by contradiction.
In particular by showing that
\mathcal{P}
does not hold for
S
implies that it does not hold either for one of the
S_{i}
’s or for
\mathbb{K}
.

A key notion for the proofs is that of
projection
of a reachable configuration of the composed system to configurations of each of the single systems
S_{i}
and also of the connection policy
\mathbb{K}
. On this basis, the most important tool to get contradictions is the subsequent
Proposition

5.4
which essentially shows that projections of reachable configurations involving no intermediate gateway states are reachable configurations again. The complete proofs of property preservations are provided in
[
6
]
.
They are independent of the communication model
\mathbb{K}
complies with.

Definition 5.3

(Configuration projections)
.

Let
S=\mathcal{M}\!\mathcal{C}\!(\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I},\mathbb{K})
be as in
Theorem

5.1

(but without no-mixed-state assumption).
Let
s=(\vec{q},\vec{w})\in\mathsf{RC}(S)
where
\vec{q}=(q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}}

and
\vec{w}=(w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\in C_{\mathbf{P}}}
.
For each
i\in I
, the projection
{s}_{\mid_{\mathbf{i}}}
of
s

to
S_{i}
is defined by

{s}_{\mid_{\mathbf{i}}}=({\vec{q}}_{\mid_{\mathbf{i}}},{\vec{w}}_{\mid_{\mathbf{i}}})

where
{\vec{q}}_{\mid_{\mathbf{i}}}=(q_{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\in\mathbf{P}_{i}}
and

{\vec{w}}_{\mid_{\mathbf{i}}}=(w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\in C_{\mathbf{P}_{i}}}
.

The projection
{s}_{\mid_{\mathbf{\mathbb{K}}}}
of
s=(\vec{q},\vec{w})
to
\mathbb{K}
is defined
if
{q}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}\not\in\widehat{Q}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}
for each
i\in I
and
then

{s}_{\mid_{\mathbf{\mathbb{K}}}}=({\vec{q}}_{\mid_{\mathbf{\mathbb{K}}}},{\vec{w}}_{\mid_{\mathbf{\mathbb{K}}}})

where
{\vec{q}}_{\mid_{\mathbf{\mathbb{K}}}}=(p_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{i}})_{i\in I}
is such that, for each
i\in I
,

p_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{i}}=\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}}
(with
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{q_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}}
being the “dotted decoration” of the local state
q_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}
)
and where

{\vec{w}}_{\mid_{\mathbf{\mathbb{K}}}}=(w^{\prime}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}})_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}\in\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{p}}}}\neq{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{q}}}}}

is such that, for each pair
{i,j\in I}
with
i\neq j
,

w^{\prime}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{i}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{j}}=w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{j}}
.

Proposition 5.4

(On reachability of projections)
.

Let
s=(\vec{q},\vec{w})\in\mathsf{RC}(S)
.

i)

For each
i\in I
, (
{q}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}\not\in\widehat{Q_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}}~\Longrightarrow~{s}_{\mid_{\mathbf{i}}}\in\mathsf{RC}(S_{i}))
;

ii)

({q}_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}\not\in\widehat{Q_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{i}}}
for each
i\in I)

~\Longrightarrow~

{s}_{\mid_{\mathbf{\mathbb{K}}}}\in\mathsf{RC}(\mathbb{K})
.

The connection policy of
Example

4.9
does enjoy all the properties of
Definition

3.4
.
Moreover, the interfaces of the four systems of
Example

4.1
are all with no mixed state.
Hence
Theorem

5.1
guarantees that
any property (among those of
Definition

3.4
, but lock-freedom) enjoyed by the systems is also enjoyed by
their PaI multicomposition.

Now we provide some examples for cases in which communication properties are
not preserved. First we show that all the three properties for which we have assumed
the no-mixed-state condition in
Theorem

5.1
would, in general, not be preserved by composition if the condition is dropped.
In the counterexamples, the receiving states introduced by the gateway construction cause the breaking of the property taken into account.

Example 5.5

(No-mixed-state counterexample for deadlock-freeness and progress preservation)
.

Let us consider the two following systems
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
with interfaces,
respectively,
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}

containing mixed states.

The initial configuration is actually a deadlock, and hence the system does also not enjoy progress.

Example 5.6

(No mixed-state counterexample for reception-error-freeness preservation)
.

S_{1}
and

with interfaces,

This communication system, however, is not reception-error free, since it is possible to reach the
configuration
s=(\vec{q},\vec{w})
where

\vec{q}=(2_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{u}}}}},2_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}},0_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}},1_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}}),\qquad w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}}=\langle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}\cdot\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}}\rangle,\qquad w_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}}=\langle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{d}}\rangle,\qquad w_{c}=\varepsilon\ \ (\forall c\not\in\{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}\})

In the configuration
s
, the CFSM
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
is in a receiving state, namely
0
, from which there are two transitions, namely

(0,{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}}?{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{c}}},\hat{0}^{\prime})
and
(0,{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}}?{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}}},\hat{0})
.
Moreover, the channels
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{v}}}}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
are both not empty and their first element
is different from both
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{b}}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{c}}
.
The above configuration is hence an unspecified reception configuration.

\diamond

Notice that
in case we dropped the requirement that
\mathbb{K}
has to comply with a communication model,
the interfaces
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
of
Example

5.6
could be simplified
to get the counterexample. In particular, they
could have just, respectively, two and three states.
The use of communication models hence limits the possibility of getting systems whose properties are not preserved by composition.
This is an indication that connection models increase
the possibility of getting safe compositions.

Let us now turn to the last communication property stated in
Definition

3.4

which is lock-freeness. This property is also meaningful in the context of synchronous
communication.

In
[
9
, Example 6.7]

a counterexample is provided, showing that in the formalism of
synchronous
CFSMs the properties of (synchronous) lock-freeness and deadlock-freeness
are, in general, not preserved.
As a matter of fact, lock-freeness is problematic also for the case of asynchronous communications and no mixed states,
as shown in the following example,
adapted from
[
9
]
.

Example 5.7

(Lock-freeness is not preserved by composition)
.

Let us consider the following communicating systems
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
.

Note that both
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are lock-free and their respective interfaces
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}
and
{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}
have no mixed states.

Let us now consider the (unique) connection policy
\mathbb{K}=(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{i}})_{i\in\{1,2\}}

where
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{1}}\in\mathsf{L}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{P}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{S}(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1}},\text{\sc cm})

and
M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{k}}}}_{2}}\in\mathsf{L}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{P}\hskip-1.0pt\mathsf{S}(M_{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}},\text{\sc cm})

with connection model
\text{\sc cm}=\{({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{x}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2}),({\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{1},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}},{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}_{2})\}
.
